**@hypersigil** (👍1 · 2009-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GLLHIiOIygw&lc=UgxWqlYa7SaIfY1epTd4AaABAg
  If you're an English professor, you must be familiar with the MLA style guide, the standard for academic writing in English. Please refer to the MLA handbook, rule 2.2.7e:
  
  "To form the possessive of any singular proper noun, add an apostrophe and an s."
  
  And the examples it gives are:
  
  Venus's beauty
  Dickens's reputation
  Descartes's philosophy
  
  Dozens of university websites confirm that this is the rule.
  
  Don't feel bad -- you learn something new every day.

  You are wrong. It is James's pen. We DO add the second "S" after the apostrophe. In the past, we did not. English is an evolving language, and this is something that has changed in the last few decades. You can check the major style guides for guidance on this issue: MLA, Chicago, etc.

  "to kill 2 birds with one stone" is in German "2 Fliegen mit einer Klappe schlagen" which means "to kill 2 flies with one swatter".   If I were / if I was
  After if and wish, you can use were instead of was (if I were / I wish it were etc.).
  If I was / I wish it was are also possible. So you can say:
  
  -  If I were you, I wouldn't buy that coat.      or If I was you, ...
  -  I'd go for a walk if it weren't so cold.         or ... if it wasn't so cold.
  -  I wish she were here.                                    or I wish she was here.
  
  end of quote. 
  
  So "I was" also possible but not common I think.

      That’s the peculiarity of the rule. We are to use ‘were’ instead of ‘was’. Always :)
    ↳ **@maksatkondo5621** (👍0 · 2019-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g9WO5iZsDn0&lc=UgiJSvjbENP4fXgCoAEC.8U2tZKviy_u8tlbUrp2TvU

    ↳ **@vickynavarroalburquerque6876** (👍0 · 2020-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g9WO5iZsDn0&lc=UgiJSvjbENP4fXgCoAEC.8U2tZKviy_u97jRLtSIRge
      because when writing sentences in an imaginary context, like second conditional for example, it's not the simple past tense what you are using BUT the subjunctive form of the verb, that's why this is the only moment when WERE  is used for all of the persons.   Phrasal verb is simply a verb with a preposition that has a greater meaning
  
  E.g. Cheer means to root for smo, to motivate or support smo, whereas Cheer up, implies making smo happy when they're sad
  
  The word UP has a couple of meanings in combination with some verbs: 
  1. Increase — add up, speak up, cheer up, jack up or make up or bring up
  2. Move — catch up, speed up, go up, walk up
  3. Complete — wrap up, finish up, end up
  
  This lesson was decent, Thank you so much Mr. James. You're a great teacher

**@PRO-qm5cl** (👍4 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YUikFw1WPEU&lc=Ugx33Y3fQa13Kxht7oN4AaABAg
  I'd like to finish this up later.
  
  PHARASAL VERB = 2 OR 3 WORDS word
  Verb + preposition (+preposition)
  
  Up + Verb = #1. Increase #2. Movement #3. Complete(ment)
  
  Don't bring up this topic as it's too sensitive for us
  
  COMPLETE:
  You've got to put gas into the tank so to fill up the fuel
  I will finish up reading my homework a bit later
  MOVE: 
  We should catch up to reach them
  If you run up to him during a race it might be possible but unlikely
  INCREASE: 
  Speak up I want to listen to you lucidly 
  Could you turn up the volume of TV set I don't here anything
  Cheer up you shouldn't be sad! You're  alive you have a home with healthy parents who care about you.
  Why don't you grow up If you're acting like a little kid
  
  THE KEY TO GET THE "UP" preposition in PHRASAL VERBS
  INCREASE MOVE AND COMPLETE
  
  There 3 uses of up in PHRASAL VERB combination: to increase, to move, to complete
  
  I want to add up some strength for my body
  Be careful you must not even walk up to this animal! Otherwise he will devour you completely!! Beware
  To wrap with this excellent lesson I need to record myself to explain clearly what I got out of this lesson
  
  Thank you Teacher James!

  Bring up = to raise children
  Fill up =to increase the volume
  Cheer up = feel better,increase happiness
  Blow up= to explode
  Finish up=to complete something later
  Turn up =to increase (usually the machine /electronic items)
  Speak up= speak louder/increase your voice
  Catch up=to increase your speed so that you move faster
  Sit up=to raise your body from lying

  A wildly inaccurate spreading of misinformation. "Have to" means you have no choice. "Must" means your are duty bound. If something "must be true" we can deduce that it is true. We can also say "it has to be true" in EXACTLY the same way. It has nothing to do with belief versus facts. As far as emotions being stronger than facts- oh dear- I detect some thinly veiled religious undertones.

**@AlexBrown-xl4lb** (👍11 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BgIjV8dkpp0&lc=UgggHWVOjd7cpXgCoAEC
  Your explanation is not quite correct. You use "must" when it is your decision (based on facts, belief, or something else - doesn't matter), i.e., it is important for you, but you have a choice not to do it.  You use "have to" when circumstances make you do something-you don't have a choice not to do it.
    ↳ **@madelyngrullon6264** (👍4 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BgIjV8dkpp0&lc=UgggHWVOjd7cpXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-EINn86TB6zDX9K4
      I think that you are wrong. I was taught that must is used wheb something is an obligation so you are no able to decide not to do it and have to you are not obliged to do it. so I think that must have a stronger obligation than have to.
    ↳ **@moniqueuu8777** (👍3 · 2017-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BgIjV8dkpp0&lc=UgggHWVOjd7cpXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-EINn8QPsHmG2dmE
      Xuan Le 
      Must is used to express what the speaker thinks. Have to us used because it is something necessary, as law or a rule. 
            In the US, I have to get a driver's license before I can drive by myself.
            I must cook tonight before the guests arrive. (not a rule or law)
      He said must is stronger than have to but I think he confused the two in the end.

      When there is some obligation and  you dont have any choice we use "have to" but if you can choose we have to use "must".

**@PRO-qm5cl** (👍0 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=94OhM6eSEPw&lc=UgzPz4FYUZrXzBZDrux4AaABAg
  Vid#4
  
  That man holds his own, but he's a bit crazy cuz he cannot hold on shouting. I mean he isn't able to hold his horses and calm down. So I decided to come up to him and say please hold your peace sir you're distracting everyone here. — I am gonna hold my ground, he replied to me
  So I told him hold his tongue and 
  to hold up there because I was about to call to the police. Then, I went to call and said that there were a man who was distracting everyone on the street, they asked me if there was a hold up but I said no. So finally they came and calmed that rebellious man down and the situation became more clear and peaceful 
  
  Hold — keep it in same place or maintain the position
  
  Hold on — wait for a moment/minute also means keeping your arms tightly around smth so you wouldn't fall down 
  
  Hold your horses — (idiom) be patient or have patience
  Hold your peace — shut up, when smo is about to be dead
  Hold your tongue — shut up
  Hold up — waiting, smth is making one wait
  Hold my own — keep up with being as good as anyone else; TO DO WELL in a difficult situation;e.g. I was holding my own even when it was hard the last summer
  Hold my ground — not gonna change your position

  It is "A European" because the sound of "Eu" sounds like it starts with a consonant, the letter Y. Same goes with the word, "University," because it sounds like it starts with the consonant letter Y. Saying "An European" or "An university" does not sound right, although they do start with a vowel, the sounds they create make the article for them change.

**@Cloudbusting2024** (👍5 · 2011-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8CiA9BCRPBk&lc=Ugxs2IaZZlKcR-9KKzB4AaABAg
  Hi James, great video but...
  
  'The' is NOT only for singular objects. 
  
  Examples:
  The dog. The dogs.  The pen. The pens. The man. The men.  Singular and plural.
  
  a & an are for singular objects.
  
  Also...
  a is followed by a consonant SOUND.    an is followed by a vowel SOUND.
  
  an hour     The h is a consonant but not a consonant sound. Hour starts with a vowel SOUND....the same as our. (Crazy English!)
  
  Thanks for THE video. It was A pleasure. THE pleasure was all mine.  :)

  There's a mistake. "The" can be use with plural objects (of course if we mean specific things). On the other hand he didn't  mention that "a" can't be use with uncountable nouns. Also "the" can't be use with the names of continents, countries, towns etc. Anyway, this lesson was very helpful for me when I didn't understand english well.

**@PRO-qm5cl** (👍1 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QMIQv7yPlkI&lc=Ugw4DwjZfp2w5FozNKt4AaABAg
  For comprehension you have to understand the context (meaning)
  IN OTHER WORDS, even if you don't understand some words you'll still be able to get the gist 
  
  #1 — Read everything 
  
  #2 — Read again with dictionary and summarize each PARAGRAPH 
  
  #3 — Read once more, this time you understand the whole article and summarize everything you've read
  
  By reading over and over you learn idioms and words, you comprehend the meaning deeper
  
  #4 — Read on daily basis anything in English as a habit

**@PRO-qm5cl** (👍0 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QMIQv7yPlkI&lc=UgxZ5AkWusvM7I_XvdZ4AaABAg
  Comprehesion is a skill of understanding 
  – One may read a book, but not truly understand the meaning
  
  Context — when you don't understand all the words and but the meaning 
  
  Get the book you need and then
  #1. FOCUS ON CONTEXT, Read quickly and underline the words you don't know (don't use the dictionary) FOCUS on the context, get the gist
  #2. READ AGAIN. Use Your Dictionary and Summarize Each Paragraph
  #3. READ IT AGAIN. Summarize Everything We've Read (you'll deeply learn the skill to cut down your reading time!!)
  
  Reading over and over again for a month you'll get YOU the real result

  #1
  
  ●Greetings:
  Hi / Hello
  
  ●How are you feeling:
  
  How are you (formal)?
  How are you doing?
  How's it hangin'? 
  What's up?
  Hey, man✋
  
  💬Responses:
  
  — I'm well / I'm fine (formal)✅
  — I'm good(adj)❌ (informal)
  
  👋
  Goodbye (formal)
  Later
  See you
